The object is a plan over whole paths, not separate shortest paths. It says where every agent is at each time and how the group moves without collisions. The planner treats the group as something that must be moved from all starts to all goals together, while obeying motion and collision rules. Name used later: constrained transport plan.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is feasible coordinated motion. Each agent should reach a valid target while preserving no-collision rules and global consistency.

Allowed Movement

The planner may temporarily ignore exact identities, solve movement for the group as a flow, or soften the path search so it has room to find a workable route. The softened view looks for likely group paths between the start cloud and goal cloud while staying near a reference motion rule. The final result still has to become real agent paths. Name used later: Schrodinger bridge.

Why It Works

This works because conflicts are handled while planning the joint movement, not patched after independent routes collide. By placing collision rules and motion rules on the whole collection of possible paths, the solver searches only for group motions that can coexist.

choose joint paths from starts to goals while satisfying motion rules and no-collision constraints

Evidence Check

Report collision rate, path cost, scaling with agent count, identity consistency, obstacle changes, start-goal perturbations, and comparison with independent planning plus repair.

Failure Boundary

The method fails when relaxed group movement cannot be assigned back to real agents, constraints are too expensive to enforce, or smoothing hides rare but catastrophic collisions.

Connection

Fair Optimal Transport constrains allocation matches. Safety-Guided Flow moves samples under constraints. HDFlow and PACT restrict embodied actions. MAPF via MMOT and Schrodinger bridges makes transport a physical planning object.

EXEMPLAR 59

Binary RAR

Factuality As No Claim Contradicts The Evidence

Problem

An answer can contain nine true sentences and one serious false one. If the score averages all ten, the answer may still look good. In high-trust retrieval, one unsupported contradiction can make the whole answer unsafe to use.

State Space

The state is the user question, the retrieved evidence, the answer's individual claims, how each claim connects to evidence, contradictions, missing support, the reward signal, and how training changes the model.

Object

The object is a pass/fail rule for the whole answer. A binary rule means there are only two outcomes: pass or fail. The rule asks whether the answer is allowed at all, not how many nice sentences surround the bad one. Name used later: admissibility predicate.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is trust in the evidence link. The model may become more fluent or complete, but it should not hide a local factual error inside many safe sentences.

Allowed Movement

The model may change wording, retrieve different evidence, abstain, or split claims more carefully. It may not get more reward by adding unsupported claims whose errors are diluted inside an average score.

Why It Works

This works because the reward no longer gives partial credit for an answer with a contradiction. The model is trained to stay inside the set of answers where every factual claim is supported and none conflict with evidence. In plain terms: one serious contradiction fails the answer.

pass only if every factual claim is supported by the retrieved evidence and not contradicted by it

Evidence Check

Evaluate contradiction rate, unsupported claims, abstention behavior, completeness, retrieval changes, adversarial evidence conflicts, and whether gains persist when evidence is replaced by harder near-contradictions.

Failure Boundary

The method fails when the claim extractor misses implied claims, retrieved evidence is incomplete or wrong, or strict pass/fail scoring makes the model over-abstain instead of using evidence better.

Connection

Omni-Reward and JudgeBench study reward as measurement. WebDevJudge checks real artifacts. CRAMF retrieves formal objects for proof. Binary RAR is the factuality version of the same principle: the checker must see the evidence relation the reward claims to measure.

EXEMPLAR 60

Conformal Policy Control

Policy Improvement As Staying Near Where Safety Was Checked

Problem

A new policy can get more reward by moving into behavior the safety data never covered. The question is not only "is this policy better?" It is also "has it moved so far from the checked behavior that our safety evidence no longer applies?"

State Space

The state is data from a reference policy, a candidate improved policy, a score for how unusual the candidate looks, a held-out calibration set, the tolerated risk level, how action patterns shift, and the final deploy-or-reject decision.

Object

The object is an acceptance region around behavior that has evidence behind it. The method uses examples that were not used to train the policy to set a cutoff for "too unusual." The accepted policies are the ones that stay close enough to checked behavior for the safety evidence to still matter. Names used later: conformal threshold for the held-out-data cutoff, and risk set for the accepted region.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is a finite-evidence guardrail around unsafe deviation. Policy improvement is allowed, but only while the candidate remains close enough to behavior for which the calibration data is relevant.

Allowed Movement

The optimized policy may differ from the reference policy. Its deviation must stay below the threshold learned from held-out safe behavior. The score is simply a measure of how unlike the checked behavior this candidate looks. Name used later: nonconformity.

Why It Works

This works by turning limited safe-behavior data into a practical cutoff. Instead of blindly trusting a learned safety model, the method asks whether the candidate looks too unusual compared with checked examples. The cutoff means something only if checked examples and future deployment cases are comparable enough to be treated as coming from the same kind of situation. Name used later: exchangeability.

accept the improved policy only when its deviation from checked behavior stays below the calibrated cutoff

Evidence Check

Report violation rate, calibration coverage, sensitivity to reference-policy quality, shift tests, risk-level sweeps, and cases where unconstrained reward optimization would leave the accepted region.

Failure Boundary

The guarantee weakens when calibration data and deployment are not comparable, when the deviation score misses the real safety variable, or when the reference policy is unsafe in rare states.

Connection

CSPO restricts movement near safety boundaries. SafeDPO shapes preference movement. Rare Event Analysis studies low-probability failures. Finite Test Certification asks what limited evidence proves. Conformal Policy Control makes policy improvement a finite-evidence constrained move.

EXEMPLAR 61

FedPissa, SmartFed, FlatLand, And FedARC

Federated Adaptation As Sharing What Is Common And Keeping What Is Local

Problem

Federated clients are not copies of one another. A phone, hospital, language community, or device may have different data, privacy limits, tasks, and compute. Averaging everyone together can erase local needs, while training everyone alone wastes shared signal.

State Space

The state is the global model, each client's local data pattern, small adapter modules, shared reference points, communication budget, private parameters, shared parameters, and each client's utility. Low-rank adapters are small trainable pieces that change the model through a limited number of direction patterns. Anchors are shared reference examples or features that help clients line up meaning.

Object

The object is a split between what should be shared and what should remain private or local. Some update directions mean the same thing across clients and can be shared. Others reflect one client's language, device, hospital, or private data and should stay local. The split matters because averaging incompatible updates can make every client worse. Name used later: decomposition of update space into shared directions, private directions, and routes chosen under resource limits.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is useful common structure without client collapse. Shared updates should improve what transfers across clients while preserving local factors that averaging would damage.

Allowed Movement

Clients may share anchors, selected adapter components, rank-wise experts, or a shared model spine. Aggregation should avoid mixing updates that mean different things for different clients.

Why It Works

This works when the system stops treating every update as directly comparable. Anchors are shared reference points that help clients line up meanings. Selective aggregation shares only compatible pieces. Routing assigns limited adapter capacity where it helps. The common idea is to make the coordinate system for sharing explicit before averaging.

share only the update parts that align across clients; keep incompatible local parts private or personalized

Evidence Check

Measure global accuracy, per-client utility, personalization gap, communication cost, signs that private information is exposed, update interference, anchor stability, and performance when client data shifts or modalities are missing.

Failure Boundary

The approach fails when anchors do not represent shared meaning, small adapter pieces store private artifacts, routing favors large clients, or personalization hides degradation for smaller clients.

Connection

RAIN-Merging and LoRA theory restrict update movement. Common Corpus makes data sources explicit. VALUEFLOW asks who benefits from aggregation. Federated adaptation asks the systems version: what can be shared without destroying what should stay local?

EXEMPLAR 62

CRAMF

Formal Retrieval As Finding The Exact Math Library Names

Problem

A proof assistant does not accept "the usual theorem about this idea." It needs the exact definition, theorem name, and symbol used in its library. A model can understand the informal idea but still fail because it chooses the wrong formal object.

State Space

The state is the informal statement, library definitions and theorem names, retrieved candidates, reranking scores, proof context, generated formal statement, and checker feedback. Mathlib is the large Lean mathematics library.

Object

The object is a narrowed set of exact formal handles the model can use. A formal handle is a library name or symbol that the proof assistant recognizes. Retrieval-conditioned means generation is guided by retrieved candidates instead of inventing names from scratch.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is meaning. The system may change informal language into formal symbols, but it must preserve the intended mathematical concept.

Allowed Movement

The system may expand the query, retrieve definitions, rerank candidates, and condition generation on them. It cannot treat nearby English words as interchangeable if the formal library treats them as different objects.

Why It Works

This works by shrinking the search before proof generation. Without retrieval, the model must guess from a huge set of discrete library names. Retrieval supplies plausible handles, and the proof checker can reject wrong ones. The hard part is the number of choices: fewer plausible names makes the search less scattered. Name used later: entropy.

map an informal concept to likely formal handles, then generate using symbols the checker can check

Evidence Check

Measure whether required definitions are retrieved, reranking precision, proof-checker acceptance, proof repair rate, near-miss definition errors, and performance when the needed concept is rare or buried deep in the library.

Failure Boundary

The method fails when retrieval misses the decisive object, the informal statement is underspecified, several library translations are valid but incompatible, or the proof checker accepts syntax that still misses the intended theorem meaning.

Connection

VERINA and WZ-LLM rely on formal checking. WebDevJudge and PaperBench evaluate native artifacts. Binary RAR ties claims to retrieved evidence. CRAMF supplies the formal-math infrastructure layer: before a proof can be checked, the system must choose the right objects to speak in.

EXEMPLAR 63

Consequence-Based Utility

Verification Through What The Claim Lets You Do Next

Problem

Some claims are hard to check directly. A theorem, method, or scientific explanation may be plausible, but the ideal checker may be too slow, unavailable, or beyond what a human can inspect.

State Space

The state is the candidate claim, downstream tasks the claim should affect, weaker available checkers, checking cost, prior evidence, and the decision that depends on whether the claim is useful or true.

Object

The object is a link from the hard claim to easier consequences. If the claim is useful or true, then certain later predictions, decisions, proof steps, or task results should change in a visible way. The map says what downstream signs should move if the claim is actually doing work. Name used later: consequence map.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is relevance. The downstream consequence must stay tied to the original claim, not become a separate easy stand-in score that can be improved while the claim itself remains unsupported.

Allowed Movement

The evaluator may use consequence checks instead of direct proof, but only if the consequence was defined before selecting the claim and is informative under stated assumptions.

Why It Works

This works when a claim has observable implications. If direct truth is hard to inspect, ask whether accepting the claim improves predictions, solves related cases, shortens later proofs, or changes decisions in ways false matched claims do not.

trust a hard claim more when pre-defined checkable consequences improve in ways false claims do not

Evidence Check

Define consequence tasks before scoring, compare against plausible false claims, report downstream effect size, test held-out consequences, and audit whether the consequence can be gamed without the original claim being valid.

Failure Boundary

The approach fails when consequences only measure impressiveness, are chosen after seeing the claim, or can be produced by a false claim through a shortcut.

Connection

HypoSpace evaluates coverage of possible explanations. RefineStat and VERINA use executable checkers. Benchmarking at the Edge of Comprehension models bounded human verification. Consequence-Based Utility covers the middle case where direct proof is missing but implications can still be tested.

EXEMPLAR 64

GraphGlue, Embedding Translation, And Diffract

Representation Transfer As Making Two Internal Languages Compatible

Problem

Two models or graph domains may both encode useful structure but use different internal coordinates. If we connect them without translation, nearby things in one system may not stay nearby in the other, and a useful direction may point somewhere wrong.

State Space

The state is multiple internal representation spaces, local domain structure, maps between embeddings, attention-head updates, confidence estimates, shared tasks, and transfer loss. An embedding is a vector representation of an item inside a model: a list of numbers used as its internal address and description.

Object

The object is a translation between internal coordinate systems. It should preserve the relationships the task uses: neighbors, directions, graph links, or useful update effects. The space may not be flat everywhere, but nearby points can still have a local coordinate map that says what counts as near, far, or directionally similar. Name used later: manifold.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is relational meaning. If two items are related, near, or directionally different in a task-relevant way, that relation should survive transfer.

Allowed Movement

The method may align local coordinate charts, translate embeddings, rewind low-value attention-head updates, or assign confidence to transfer. It must preserve the relations used by the target task.

Why It Works

This works when the translation preserves structure instead of only matching average positions. GraphGlue lines up graph domains through local charts. Embedding Translation learns pairwise maps and confidence. Diffract asks which attention-head updates add value and which can be removed. The shared point is that internal coordinates are not automatically interchangeable.

translate representations so task-relevant relationships remain true after the map

Evidence Check

Measure neighborhood preservation, cross-domain transfer, confidence outside the training domain, error after chained translations, attention-head removal or rewind effects, and cases where global alignment hides local distortion.

Failure Boundary

The approach fails when no shared structure exists, alignment preserves easy geometry but loses the target variable, or confidence scores miss rare regions where translation errors compound.

Connection

FedARC uses anchors for federated alignment. Alignment-Sensitive Minimax Rates asks whether signal sits in recoverable directions. RAIN-Merging restricts adaptation subspaces. GraphGlue, Embedding Translation, and Diffract ask which coordinates can move across systems without changing meaning.

EXEMPLAR 65

MetamerGen

Human Perception As Different Images That Feel The Same

Problem

Two images can differ in pixels while a person reports seeing the same scene. This happens because human vision does not preserve every pixel equally: central fixation, peripheral gist, attention, and task all matter.

State Space

The state is the image, where the person looks, central and peripheral visual information, the internal percept, the generated image, human same-different judgment, and model perceptual score. Foveal means central high-detail vision; peripheral means outside the center of gaze.

Object

The object is the set of physically different images that a person judges as the same under a viewing condition. The plain rule is: different images can belong in one group when they count as the same for human perception.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is the human scene percept, not the exact pixels or a generic image-model score. The generator should change details people do not use while preserving what they do use.

Allowed Movement

The model may change the image through a hidden generation process, but the change is valid only if it stays inside the tested human same-percept group. A metamer is one of these physically different but perceptually matching stimuli.

Why It Works

This works by turning a theory of perception into a generator constraint. Instead of asking only whether an image looks realistic, it asks which changes humans fail to notice. That makes generation a probe of what information human vision actually preserves.

two images are metamers when humans judge the same percept under the viewing condition even though the pixels differ

Evidence Check

Use same-different human judgments, fixation-conditioned tests, peripheral-detail changes, model-human disagreement analysis, and controls showing ordinary image similarity is not enough to explain acceptance.

Failure Boundary

The method fails when generated images exploit task artifacts, participant judgments are noisy or biased, or the model preserves low-level statistics without preserving the intended scene percept.

Connection

PRISM and Mind-Omni build interfaces to human signals. DAVE and Visual Attribution Streaming ask which visual evidence caused an answer. Depth Anything 3 recovers geometric variables. MetamerGen adds the human-perception object: what visual information can change without changing experience?

EXEMPLAR 66

Fair Causal Bandits

Exploration As Learning Without Taking Unfair Actions

Problem

A system that learns by trying actions can cause unfair harm while it is still learning. It is not enough to check fairness after learning is done. Some exploratory actions may already be illegitimate.

State Space

The state is the context, action, reward, cause assumptions, protected attribute, possible deliberate change to that attribute, uncertain fairness rule, confidence set, regret, and history of violations. A bandit is a repeated decision problem where the system learns rewards by trying actions.

Object

The object is an exploration rule that only chooses actions allowed by the fairness evidence so far. The fairness question asks what would have happened to the same case if a sensitive condition, such as a protected attribute, had been different. It is a same-person, changed-condition question, not a comparison between two unrelated people. Name used later: counterfactual.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is legitimacy of action, not only total reward. The learner should improve reward while bounding the chance or size of unfairness under the protected change being considered.

Allowed Movement

The policy may try uncertain actions only when the current evidence range and cause assumptions say the action is plausibly fair, or when the algorithm explicitly accounts for the violation risk.

Why It Works

This works by making fairness part of the decision rule while learning happens. Exploration is no longer free information gathering. It is movement inside an allowed set whose boundary is updated from causal evidence.

try high-reward actions only when the estimated unfairness under the protected change stays below the allowed risk

Evidence Check

Report reward regret and fairness violations together, test wrong causal graphs, vary protected-attribute interventions, inspect conservative or abstention regions, and compare with methods that audit fairness only after learning.

Failure Boundary

The guarantee fails when the causal graph omits key paths, sensitive-attribute interventions are not well defined, confidence intervals are miscalibrated, or fairness constraints depend on facts the system cannot observe.

Connection

TRECA abstains when treatment response is uncertain. Fair Optimal Transport restricts matches. VALUEFLOW exposes who loses under aggregation. Conformal Policy Control bounds policy movement from safe behavior. Fair Causal Bandits bring the same principle into exploration: valid learning must respect the constraint while information is incomplete.

EXEMPLAR 67

CreDRO, CRC, And MIRA

Uncertainty As The Set Of Actions Still Justified

Problem

A model can give a well-calibrated confidence number and still not tell a doctor, operator, or classifier what to do. The useful question is not only "how unsure are we?" It is "which actions are still safe or justified given what we do not know?"

State Space

The state is the input, possible labels or possible output distributions, the set of remaining plausible answers, the set of allowed predictions, the cost of wrong actions, abstention, calibration data, and possible deployment shift.

Object

The object is a set of possibilities shaped by the decision it supports. Instead of forcing one confidence number, the method keeps several still-plausible ways the world might be and asks which actions remain acceptable under all of them. The set is useful only if it keeps the alternatives that would change the action. Names used later: credal set for a collection of possible probability models, conformal set for a finite-data prediction set chosen from held-out examples, and regional mass agreement for checking whether probability is placed correctly in the parts of the answer space where the decision is sensitive.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is action validity. The uncertainty set should keep enough alternatives to avoid unsafe overconfidence, but remove enough impossible alternatives that a useful action remains.

Allowed Movement

The method may widen, shrink, or reshape the remaining possibilities when evidence changes decision risk. The set can grow when the situation is unfamiliar and shrink when evidence rules out alternatives. Coverage, meaning how often the true answer is included, is not enough if the set is so broad that every action becomes equally bad.

Why It Works

This works because the uncertainty is built for the decision that will consume it. If every plausible answer in the set leads to the same safe action, the system can act. If plausible answers disagree in a way that changes the action, the system should abstain, ask for more evidence, or choose a robust action.

act only when every plausible answer in the uncertainty set makes the action acceptably safe

Evidence Check

Report how often the set includes the right answer, set size, value of abstention, damage under cautious decisions, behavior outside the training mix, probability placement in decision-sensitive regions, and cases where ordinary confidence is calibrated but recommends the wrong action.

Failure Boundary

The approach fails when the uncertainty set leaves out the real shift, is so broad that no useful decision remains, or is calibrated on data whose costs do not match deployment.

Connection

LIMSSR keeps missing information explicit. Rare Event Analysis samples tail uncertainty. Conformal Policy Control bounds policy movement. Fair Causal Bandits constrain exploration. CreDRO, CRC, and MIRA make uncertainty useful by tying it to the decision.

EXEMPLAR 68

Floating-Point Neural Networks

Expressivity As What The Machine Can Actually Store And Compute

Problem

A theorem may say a network can represent a function using perfect real numbers. Real hardware does not use perfect real numbers. It stores rounded numbers with limited digits, so some delicate constructions disappear when the model is actually run.

State Space

The state is the network depth and width, activation function, number format, storable weights, rounding rule, intermediate layer values, and the target function. Floating point is the common machine format for approximate real numbers. A mantissa is the stored significant digits of such a number.

Object

The object is the set of input-output rules a network can actually carry when every number is stored on real hardware. The question is not "could the math work with infinitely precise numbers?" It is "can the deployed machine still store the needed thresholds, additions, and comparisons after rounding?" Name used later: expressivity under finite precision.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is the claimed computation after every operation is performed in the deployed number system. The proof should survive rounding, overflow, underflow, and limited precision.

Allowed Movement

The proof may change depth, width, precision, or representation. It must count which values can actually be stored and how every operation transforms them.

Why It Works

This works by moving the theorem onto the machine that runs the model. Perfect-real-number proofs can rely on tiny gaps, exact thresholds, or cancellations that need unlimited digits. Real machines round after each operation, so a gap that looked decisive on paper can vanish or flip. Floating-point analysis asks whether the same behavior remains when every layer uses those rounded values.

the implemented network must approximate the target after every operation uses the finite number format

Evidence Check

State precision assumptions, test different formats and accumulation orders, compare ideal constructions to implemented networks, and report input-output rules that work with perfect numbers but disappear after machine rounding.

Failure Boundary

The result can still miss deployment if hardware routines round differently, mixed precision changes intermediate states, or the theorem covers possible computation but not learning from finite data.

Connection

LiftQuant and TetraJet study low-bit approximation. Quantized diffusion follows accumulated numerical error. Softmax Turing asks what attention can compute in principle. Floating-Point Neural Networks adds the deployment caveat: in principle is not enough unless the arithmetic carries the computation.

EXEMPLAR 69

Data Market Pricing And Bayesian Truthful Valuation

Data Value As Paying For Information That Actually Helps

Problem

Data is not valuable just because there is a lot of it. Sellers can duplicate examples, hide useful data, exaggerate labels, coordinate with each other, or sell evidence that overlaps with someone else's. A pricing rule that ignores behavior may pay for noise instead of useful information.

State Space

The state is the data sellers, what each privately knows, reusable data assets, buyer value, model improvement from one more source, seller reports, payments, strategic actions, and the final market outcome.

Object

The object is a market rule for information. It has to answer three plain questions: what did this seller add that the buyer did not already have, what could the seller gain by lying, and can the same data be reused by others without being used up? The payment rule must make useful honesty more attractive than exaggeration or duplicate selling. Names used later: mechanism for the report-to-payment rule, incentive-compatible for making honest useful contribution the sensible strategy, and non-rival for data that can be reused without being consumed.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is true extra evidence value. The market should pay for data that changes downstream usefulness, not duplicated volume or metadata that is easy to manipulate.

Allowed Movement

The rule may price, rank, screen, or combine data sources. It must account for seller incentives and for the fact that information can be copied and reused. A payment rule that ignores overlap pays twice for the same evidence; a rule that ignores strategy rewards whatever is easiest to exaggerate.

Why It Works

This works by changing the game around data contribution. If payment is tied to the real improvement a source adds after other data are considered, and if honest reporting is rewarded, sellers have less reason to distort what they provide. The relevant value is not total dataset size; it is the extra usefulness this source adds on top of what is already available. Name used later: marginal value.

pay for the extra buyer value caused by a truthful useful contribution, not for raw volume

Evidence Check

Test truthful-report stability, duplicated or correlated sources, collusion, distribution shift, buyer-specific utility, and whether measured data value predicts real downstream gain after other data are included.

Failure Boundary

The market rule fails when real sellers can act outside the model, buyer utility is misspecified, data sources are secretly correlated, or payments reward a stand-in benchmark instead of buyer value.

Connection

Sequential Data Values studies conditional contribution. Common Corpus makes source history auditable. Accuracy Auctions price prediction value. VALUEFLOW exposes value aggregation. Data Market Pricing and Bayesian Truthful Valuation make the incentive layer explicit.

EXEMPLAR 70

CompSLOT, M-CBE, And Falling Trees

Interpretability As Making The Prediction Go Through Inspectable Parts

Problem

An explanation can be a caption written after the model has already decided. That is weak. In high-stakes or continually learning systems, the explanation should shape the path by which the prediction is made.

State Space

The state is the input features, learned concepts or object slots, the map from concepts to task output, expert expressions, tree or rule structure, task stream, prediction, and whether the explanation stays stable under shift.

Object

The object is a predictor with inspectable middle steps. Before the final answer, the model has to expose the parts of the situation it is using: color, shape, symptom, object, rule branch, or expert formula. Concepts are those named properties. Slots are reusable containers for parts or objects. A concept bottleneck means the model must pass through those concept variables before predicting, so the explanation is on the path to the answer instead of written afterward.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is the link between the exposed middle step and the final decision. If the model says "wheel present" mattered, changing that named part should change the prediction in the claimed way. That is functional interpretability: the visible explanation actually carries part of the computation.

Allowed Movement

The model may learn concepts, combine slots, route through expert formulas, or grow tree branches. The prediction should remain expressible through the stated inspectable structure.

Why It Works

This works when the bottleneck is real. CompSLOT uses object-centered slots so stable parts can be reused. M-CBE lets concept bottlenecks use several expert expressions instead of one brittle linear rule. Falling Trees grows ordered rules while preserving risk reasoning. Interpretability helps only when it narrows the model to structures that carry task-relevant evidence.

the explanation is real when changing the named concept changes the prediction as claimed

Evidence Check

Intervene on concepts, test concept stability under shift, measure continual retention, compare explanation sparsity with predictive loss, and show that changing a named concept changes the prediction in the expected direction.

Failure Boundary

The approach fails when concepts are correlated stand-ins, humans misread exposed variables, flexible experts hide uninterpretable interactions, or the bottleneck removes information needed for the task.

Connection

MoCA routes multimodal failure to perception or reasoning. Neural Effect Search turns features into testable hypotheses. MetamerGen tests human perceptual equivalence. CompSLOT, M-CBE, and Falling Trees make interpretability a model constraint instead of a caption.

EXEMPLAR 71

Benchmarking At The Edge Of Comprehension

Evaluation As Checking Only What The Checker Can Actually See

Problem

For hard outputs, a person may not be able to verify the whole answer. They may only check a few local claims, contradictions, or consequences. A benchmark that assumes unlimited human checking is measuring an imaginary process.

State Space

The state is the model output, smaller local claims, human checker capacity, adversarial challenges, checking budget, remaining uncertainty, and benchmark score.

Object

The object is a checking process with limited inspectors. The benchmark must say which pieces the checker can inspect, how much time or skill the checker has, and what a local check proves about the whole answer. The setup is a game in the plain sense: the model can choose what to show, the checker can choose what to inspect, and the rules decide what evidence counts. Name used later: verification game.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is evidence for correctness under limited checking. The benchmark should distinguish a claim that was checked from one that merely sounded coherent.

Allowed Movement

The evaluator may sample subclaims, ask local questions, use adversarial probes, or defer uncertain judgments. It cannot turn local checks into a global truth claim unless it states the assumption connecting parts to the whole.

Why It Works

This works by matching the score to the checker's actual view. If humans can inspect only local pieces, the score should say what those local checks imply, not pretend the whole artifact was directly verified.

score correctness through checked local claims plus stated assumptions about how those checks cover the whole answer

Evidence Check

Report checker agreement, local-claim coverage, adversarially selected failures, uncertainty intervals, and examples where fluent global answers pass surface review but fail local checks.

Failure Boundary

The method fails when local checks miss global inconsistency, humans cannot judge sampled claims reliably, or the model learns to hide hard errors between inspected regions.

Connection

Consequence-Based Utility checks downstream implications. Finite Test Certification bounds finite evidence. WebDevJudge checks native artifacts. Benchmarking at the Edge of Comprehension adds the human-verifier limit: evaluation has to model what the checker can actually see.

EXEMPLAR 72

HSD And Global Resolution

Fast Decoding As Accepting Draft Words Without Changing The Model Being Sampled

Problem

A smaller draft model can suggest words quickly, and the larger target model can check them. This can speed generation. But if accepted drafts change which outputs are likely, the system is no longer sampling from the target model.

State Space

The state is the target model's next-token probabilities, draft proposals, several possible continuations, the accept-or-reject rule, leftover probability after rejection, latency budget, and deviation from target behavior.

Object

The object is a joint rule connecting draft words to the words the target model would have produced by itself. The draft model is allowed to save time only when the final odds of each possible next word still match the target model, or when the mismatch is explicitly bounded. The rule ties the cheap draft process to the expensive target process so accepted words can be compared against what the target would have sampled. Name used later: coupling.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is output fidelity. The generated sequence should be distributed like direct target-model decoding, or differ only by a controlled amount.

Allowed Movement

The algorithm may propose multiple drafts, reorder checks, solve allocation subproblems, and reuse cheap model work. Every accepted token must still be corrected against the target probabilities.

Why It Works

This works when the acceptance rule exactly accounts for the difference between the draft model and the target model. If the small model over-suggests a word, the checker must accept it less often; if rejection leaves probability behind, that leftover probability must be redistributed correctly. In plain terms, samples made under the cheap rule are corrected so the final output still follows the target rule. Name used later: change-of-measure correction.

accept draft tokens only with a rule that keeps final output probabilities equal or close to the target model

Evidence Check

Measure speedup, acceptance rate, divergence from target decoding, rare-token behavior, long-sequence drift, multi-draft interactions, and downstream quality under fixed target-model settings.

Failure Boundary

The method fails when small local deviations accumulate, drafts miss rare important tokens, or implementation shortcuts break the acceptance rule.

Connection

Quantized diffusion tracks accumulated sampler error. Safety-Guided Flow changes paths deliberately. ThunderAgent speculates over actions. HSD and Global Resolution are the decoding version: approximate computation is allowed only when the target distribution survives.

EXEMPLAR 73

Secret Memory Lower Bounds, WGM Domain Discovery, And High-Dimensional Gaussian Mechanism

Privacy As Learning Population Facts Without Exposing One Person

Problem

Privacy is not just "add noise." The real question is what can still be discovered when individual people must remain protected, memory is limited, and the system may not even know all possible items ahead of time.

State Space

The state is user-level data, unknown item domain, private summaries, memory budget, noise rule, attacker model, utility target, and the population-level set being discovered.

Object

The object is a protected discovery channel. Many people's records should still reveal a population fact, such as which items exist or which pattern is common, while one person's record should not leave a recognizable fingerprint. The release rule deliberately adds chance so the output is not too tied to any single user. Name used later: randomized map.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is population signal under individual protection. The method should reveal what many users support without making one user's rare item or path identifiable.

Allowed Movement

The algorithm may compress data into sketches, add noise, discover missing domain mass, or change the noise shape. It must respect privacy and memory limits at the same time.

Why It Works

This works by treating privacy as a limit on how much one person can move the release. If adding or removing one user barely changes what outsiders see, that user's record is harder to detect. The difficulty is that hiding individuals can also hide the population signal, especially when data arrives as a stream, the item list is unknown, or the useful pattern lives across many dimensions. Names used later: differential privacy for the one-person-change guarantee, lower bound for proof that some private tasks need at least a certain amount of memory, domain discovery for finding unknown items, and Gaussian mechanism for adding bell-shaped noise.

release population information while making any one user's contribution hard to detect within the memory budget

Evidence Check

Report privacy settings, memory needs as the problem grows, usefulness on rare items, recovery of previously unknown items, finite-regime noise comparisons, attacks using outside information, and whether downstream artifacts leak information outside the private release rule.

Failure Boundary

The guarantee fails when deployed artifacts store data outside the private channel, side information changes the attack, or the utility target requires information privacy intentionally removed.

Connection

Gaussian Certified Unlearning defines deletion as indistinguishability. Common Corpus audits source history. Catch-22 frames watermarking as evidence testing. Secret Memory, WGM, and high-dimensional mechanisms state the resource side: privacy changes what can be discovered and stored.

EXEMPLAR 74

Learning-Augmented Paging And Matroid Algorithms

Advice And Oracle Calls As Help That Still Has A Price

Problem

An algorithm can look strong if predictions are treated as free and always helpful, or if a special query is treated as constant cost. In real use, predictions can be wrong, and a query can become expensive when the object being queried is large.

State Space

The state is the online request sequence, machine-learned advice, how much the algorithm trusts that advice, worst-case fallback, helper-query access model, query size, algorithm decision, and realized cost. Name used later: oracle, an abstract helper that answers a specific kind of query.

Object

The object is a guarantee that prices every helper. The algorithm can use a prediction or a special query, but the proof must say what happens when the prediction is wrong and how expensive the query really was. It states performance as a function of prediction use, prediction error, helper-query cost, and fallback behavior when advice is bad.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is robustness. Good predictions may improve performance, but bad predictions should not destroy worst-case protection, and oracle calls should not hide real work.

Allowed Movement

The algorithm may use learned advice or helper queries. It must budget how much it trusts advice and count each helper call in the units the deployed system actually pays.

Why It Works

This works when help is explicit rather than magical. Learning-augmented algorithms use predictions while keeping a fallback guarantee. Helper-query analysis becomes realistic when query cost is charged by what the machine really has to inspect. The independence structure says which choices can coexist, like selecting a feasible set under exchange rules. Name used later: matroid.

use advice when it helps, fall back safely when it is wrong, and count each query at its real cost

Evidence Check

Measure performance across prediction error, trust budgets, adversarial request sequences, natural helper-query sizes, and cases where treating helper calls as free reverses the practical algorithm ranking.

Failure Boundary

The approach fails when prediction errors concentrate on hard cases, the trust budget is tuned after seeing results, or the real helper has side costs missing from the model.

Connection

ParetoPO budgets tool calls. ATLAS spends evaluation questions by information. Benchmarking at the Edge models verifier limits. TileLang counts memory movement. Learning-Augmented Paging and Matroid Algorithms add the theory version: every helpful external resource needs a price in the guarantee.

EXEMPLAR 75

Distribution Transformer And Gaussian Mixture Distances

Updating Beliefs Without Flattening Them

Problem

Imagine sorting reports about an unknown machine fault. Before opening the reports, you may have several live stories: maybe the battery is weak, maybe a sensor is loose, maybe two small problems are interacting. New evidence should make some stories stronger and others weaker. It should not force all uncertainty into one neat answer just because a formula or model wants a single center.

State Space

The state is the set of possible stories before the evidence, how much trust each story gets, the new observations, the possibility that some observations are dirty or misleading, the revised set of stories after seeing the evidence, and the decision that will be made from that revised belief.

Object

The object is a belief-updating machine. Start with a table full of possible stories, each with a note saying how believable it is. New evidence should not merely pick one story. It should move the notes: this story now fits better, this one fits worse, this one is still possible but needs a wider margin. In math language, that table is a probability distribution. When the possible stories form several rounded piles rather than one pile, the formal handle is a mixture of Gaussians. Distribution Transformer learns how evidence moves the whole table. Gaussian Mixture Distances supplies the measuring tape for asking whether two such tables are close or meaningfully different.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is the meaning of the separate possibilities. If three explanations were genuinely different before the evidence, the update has to keep track of which explanation gained support, which lost support, and which remains uncertain. The method is not allowed to hide several live explanations inside one overconfident average.

Allowed Movement

What may change is the weight, location, spread, and interaction of the possible explanations. A story can become more likely, less likely, broader, narrower, or connected to another story. It should disappear only when the evidence really rules it out.

Why It Works

It works because the update is forced to respect two jobs at once. First, each possible story must be checked against the new evidence. Second, the stories must still be compared with each other, because evidence that weakens one story may strengthen another nearby story or may split one rough story into two sharper ones. The method helps only if it keeps that bookkeeping visible. The distance measure is the audit: it asks whether the changed belief still has the right separate piles, the right spread inside each pile, and the right amount of trust on each pile. Name used later: the revised belief after evidence is the posterior.

belief before evidence -> observe data -> belief after evidence, while keeping separate possibilities and measuring how much the belief cloud moved

Evidence Check

Check whether the final belief gives honest confidence, recovers the right separate explanations, assigns sensible weights, stays robust when some samples are contaminated, makes good downstream decisions, and keeps two possible answers separate when the evidence does not justify choosing one.

Failure Boundary

The method fails when the allowed family of belief shapes is too limited, when the labels for the separate possibilities stop meaning anything, or when the learned updater has only practiced on clean simulated evidence and then meets messier real evidence.

Connection

CreDRO and CRC keep uncertainty as sets of defensible actions. Mind-Omni builds a translation layer for hidden biological signals. Alignment-Sensitive Rates ask what signal can be found with finite evidence. Distribution Transformer and Gaussian Mixture Distances give the belief version: learning is not only choosing an answer, but moving uncertainty in a way that keeps its shape meaningful.

EXEMPLAR 76

Auxiliary MCMC, Path-Dependent DAI, And p-less

Drawing Examples Without Losing The Rules

Problem

Suppose you want a fair picture of a city by visiting neighborhoods. If you only visit the easy streets, your picture is biased. If you take shortcuts without recording them, you cannot tell whether the final map is fair. Sampling has the same problem: a generated example can look reasonable even when the route used to get it distorted what was supposed to be sampled.

State Space

The state is the thing we want to sample from, the rule for suggesting the next move, any extra helper variables, the path already taken, the rule for accepting or rejecting a move, the cutoff for which choices are allowed, the amount of exploration, and the variety of samples produced.

Object

The object is a path-aware drawing rule. Imagine drawing names from a jar, except every draw changes which names are easier to reach next. To be fair, the method cannot only show the final name. It also has to remember how that name became reachable. A one-step-at-a-time sampler is called a Markov chain. Estimating an answer from many such draws is called Monte Carlo. Auxiliary MCMC adds helper facts so hard moves become easier. Path-Dependent DAI keeps the construction route as part of the sample. p-less changes how much of the low-probability tail remains open instead of using one fixed cutoff everywhere.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is the intended mix of outcomes. Exploration is helpful only if the final pile of samples still represents the target source, or if the method clearly says it is sampling from a changed source.

Allowed Movement

What may change is the route. The sampler can add helper variables, remember earlier steps, raise or lower cutoffs, or take bigger exploratory moves. But every such move has to be paid for in the accounting: the method must know how that route changes the chance of ending at the sample.

Why It Works

It works because a sample has two meanings: what it is, and how likely the method was to produce it. If two routes can land on the same example but one route is much easier, the final pile will be skewed unless that route advantage is corrected. Helper variables can open doors into regions that ordinary moves rarely reach. Path memory prevents the sampler from pretending all arrivals were equally natural. Adaptive cutoffs keep extra choices alive when the model is unsure and narrow the choice set when the next step is already clear. The plain rule is: explore freely, but keep the receipt for how each sample was made.

valid sampling = proposed example + route information + correction rule, so the final sample pile still matches the intended source

Evidence Check

Check whether samples match the intended source, how often proposed moves are accepted, how many genuinely different examples are produced, whether quality and diversity trade off cleanly, what happens when path memory is removed, and whether rare but valid outcomes remain reachable.

Failure Boundary

The method fails when helper variables add noise without reaching new regions, when the needed history is too large to track reliably, or when an adaptive cutoff removes rare words or actions that matter for reasoning, creativity, or safety.

Connection

HSD and Global Resolution accept draft tokens only when they preserve the intended generation rule. Safety-Guided Flow changes generation paths on purpose. MAPF moves many agents along paths without collisions. Auxiliary MCMC, Path-Dependent DAI, and p-less make the shared point: the route is part of the evidence, not decoration.

EXEMPLAR 77

Generative Filtering And Linear Recurrent Memory

Remembering Just Enough To Predict What Comes Next

Problem

Think of tracking a delivery truck when you only see it at a few intersections. The latest sighting is not enough; you also need what earlier sightings imply about its hidden route. A model in a partly observed world has the same problem. It must remember enough of the past to know what might happen next, without carrying every raw detail forever.

State Space

The state is the stream of observations, the hidden situation those observations only partly reveal, a cloud of possible hidden situations or a compact memory, the rule for how the situation changes, the rule for what can be observed, the action or prediction being made, and the updated belief after each new observation.

Object

The object is a compact memory that is good enough for the future. Picture a dispatcher tracking a truck from scattered sightings. The dispatcher does not need every camera frame forever; they need the current set of live route guesses, how likely each one is, and what the next sighting should look like under each guess. A memory with exactly that needed information is called a sufficient belief state. Generative Filtering learns how to push the live guesses forward when time passes. Linear Recurrent Memory asks when a simple running note can carry the same useful information without storing the whole past.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is uncertainty that matters later. The memory can forget the color of a truck if that never changes the route estimate, but it cannot forget a rare turn that would make the next correct action different.

Allowed Movement

What may change is the form of the memory. The method can use many particles, a learned simulator, or a simple recurrent vector. The form is flexible; the job is not. It must keep the hidden possibilities that affect future observations or decisions.

Why It Works

It works only when forgotten details truly cannot change the next decision. If two different histories lead to the same useful memory, then every future test the task cares about should treat those histories the same. That is the deep reason compression is allowed. Generative Filtering helps by carrying several hidden guesses forward instead of committing too early. Linear Recurrent Memory helps when the world has a repeatable rule that can be tracked by updating one running note at each step. Name used later: filtering means repeatedly revising hidden-state guesses as new observations arrive.

good memory at time t means: future depends on the remembered belief, not on every raw observation before t

Evidence Check

Compare the compact memory to using the full history, test whether hidden states are recovered, insert delayed clues, measure whether the set of possible states collapses too early, remove memory dimensions, and test actions when the current observation alone is ambiguous.

Failure Boundary

The method fails when the memory drops a rare hidden possibility, when the learned simulator changes how uncertainty should evolve, or when the recurrent memory predicts common cases well while forgetting the hidden case that changes the decision.

Connection

ThinKV and EntroKV keep only context that will matter later. Mamba Markov ICL uses recurrence to infer the source of a sequence. VideoKR keeps temporal evidence. Generative Filtering and Linear Recurrent Memory give the control version: memory matters when it carries the hidden state needed for the next move.

EXEMPLAR 78

Unpaired Causal IV And Evolutionary Selection

Using Natural Differences To Learn What Caused What

Problem

Suppose one city changes bus schedules and another changes school hours, but you do not have perfect person-by-person records. You still want to know which change affected commute time. The danger is that the cities may differ in many other ways. Causal work starts by asking which differences are useful clues and which differences are distractions.

State Space

The state is the environment where data came from, samples that may not be paired person-by-person, the exposure or treatment, the outcome, the process that selected which cases appear, hidden causes that were not measured, assumptions about which changes are allowed to act as clues, and the causal effect being estimated.

Object

The object is useful natural variation. Think of a school lottery: winning the lottery may change whether a child attends a program, while the lottery number itself should not directly change the child's later test score. That outside nudge can help separate cause from background differences. The method is useful only when the nudge changes the thing we care about and leaves the other routes alone. That kind of nudge is called an instrumental variable. In evolutionary selection, the outside nudge is repeated survival or copying: traits that keep lasting under a rule reveal what the rule favored.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is the meaning of the causal question: what would change if we changed the treatment itself? The environment label or selection process is useful only if it helps isolate that question, not if it merely describes where the data came from.

Allowed Movement

What may change is the environment, the mix of samples, or the selection pressure over time. But the method must say exactly what is assumed: the outside change must affect the outcome through the intended path, it must actually move the treatment, and the underlying relation must stay stable enough to compare.

Why It Works

It works by finding a difference that behaves like a clean lever. A clean lever does not prove causality by magic; it earns trust by passing three plain tests. It must actually move the possible cause. It must not directly move the outcome through a side door. And the groups being compared must be close enough that the lever is the main relevant difference. When those tests hold, rival stories are forced to give the same answer about the target effect. Name used later: identification means the data plus assumptions leave only one answer to the causal question.

causal answer is credible when the useful outside change moves the cause, leaves other paths alone, and forces compatible stories to agree

Evidence Check

Check whether the outside lever is strong, whether it might affect the outcome directly, whether the result holds in new environments, whether simulated selection recovers the known effect, whether negative controls stay unchanged, and whether the answer changes when pairing assumptions are weakened.

Failure Boundary

The method fails when environments directly affect outcomes, when selection changes the mechanism over time, when unpaired samples come from mismatched populations, or when an unmeasured cause can explain the same pattern.

Connection

Distributional Equivalence warns that different causal stories can fit the same observations. Local Covariate Selection asks which facts are needed for one fair comparison. CausalGame gathers active evidence. Unpaired Causal IV and Evolutionary Selection show the passive-data version: natural structure helps only when its assumptions are explicit enough to rule out rival stories.

EXEMPLAR 79

FlowGuard, DeceptionDecoded, And DOUBT

Checking Which Clue Made The Model Unsafe

Problem

A picture alone can be harmless. A sentence alone can be harmless. Together they can give a model enough context to produce a dangerous instruction, a false accusation, or a misleading explanation. Safety therefore cannot only ask whether the image is safe and whether the text is safe. It has to ask what happened when the clues were combined.

State Space

The state is the visual clue, the text clue, the model's combined understanding, the answer from image alone, the answer from text alone, the answer from both, uncertainty about objects, uncertainty about relationships between objects, and pressure from attacks or misinformation.

Object

The object is a map of evidence routes: which clue pushed the answer, and how much. If a label on a bottle, a nearby caption, and a user's question jointly create a risky request, the checker needs to know which part supplied which meaning. FlowGuard separates danger that came from the image, the text, their shared meaning, their interaction, or one channel drowning out the other. DeceptionDecoded asks whether the model relied on real source evidence or a cheap surface cue. DOUBT separates two kinds of uncertainty: not knowing what object is present, and knowing the objects but not knowing their relationship.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is grounded support. If an answer claims something, the system should still be able to point to the image, text, or combination that supports it. If the dangerous meaning appears only after fusion, that route should be visible before the model turns it into a fluent answer.

Allowed Movement

What may change is the response strategy. The system may combine clues, refuse, ask for clarification, warn, or route the case to another checker. But it must keep enough route information to say which clue changed the decision and whether that change was justified.

Why It Works

It works because a safety failure often starts before the final answer is written. The model first builds meaning from pieces of evidence. If changing only the image flips the answer, the image route carried the decision. If changing only the text flips it, the text route carried it. If neither route alone is enough but together they create the risky meaning, the interaction is the problem. If the answer follows layout, style, or an attack pattern instead of grounded evidence, the route is not trustworthy. Name used later: information decomposition means splitting one decision into the evidence channels that carried it.

risk is located by changing one clue at a time and asking which route actually caused the unsafe answer

Evidence Check

Run image-only, text-only, and fused tests; swap the image while keeping the text; swap the text while keeping the image; test misleading cues; separate object mistakes from relationship mistakes; and check whether the warning signal appears before the final answer becomes unsafe.

Failure Boundary

The method fails when attacks imitate normal image-text dependence, when route scores are trained only on familiar jailbreaks, or when the model uses a hidden meaning that the route map does not represent.

Connection

MoCA, VGS, Causal Route Gating, VISUALSWAP, and MINT ask whether visual evidence actually caused an answer. FlowGuard, DeceptionDecoded, and DOUBT add the safety question: did the route that caused the answer have real support, and did it stay safe under pressure?

EXEMPLAR 80

Information Flow UQ, DLMR, RFCMH, WETR, And AGREE

Keeping Receipts For Every Claim

Problem

A research assistant may answer from a retrieved article, a stored memory, a weak label, a chart, a previous summary, or its own guess. If all of those sources are dumped into one context, the assistant can start treating a guess as if it were evidence. The mistake then persists because the system has lost the receipt for where the claim came from.

State Space

The state is retrieved passages, context tokens, visual or multimodal observations, memory writes, weak labels, trust weights, generated claims, which internal layers used which evidence, and the confidence attached to the final answer.

Object

The object is an evidence ledger with arrows and trust levels. Each claim should carry a source tag: directly observed, copied from a retrieved passage, inferred from several sources, remembered from an earlier step, weakly labeled by a noisy process, or unsupported. The arrows say which source helped create which claim. The trust level says how much that source deserves to count. Information Flow UQ traces which context pieces actually influenced a retrieval-augmented answer. DLMR keeps multimodal observations separate from derived memory. RFCMH repairs unsupported multimodal generations by checking consistency. WETR and AGREE adjust weak labels according to how reliable they appear to be.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is source identity. A claim should still be marked as observed, retrieved, inferred, weakly labeled, corrected, or unsupported after the model compresses and uses it.

Allowed Movement

What may change is how evidence is stored and combined. The model may retrieve, summarize, compress, fuse modalities, repair a claim, or write memory. It may not erase the difference between a primary observation, a source quote, a model inference, and a weak hint.

Why It Works

It works because confidence should come from surviving evidence, not from fluent wording. A passage should count only if removing it would weaken the claim. A memory should count only if it still points back to what was originally seen or read. A weak label should count less when similar labels have often been wrong. A repaired answer should say what evidence repaired it. The deeper rule is that compression and reasoning are allowed to rearrange evidence, but not to erase its origin or strength. Names used later: provenance means source tracking, and uncertainty quantification means honest confidence.

accept a claim when enough reliable sources point to it and their source labels survive retrieval, memory, and compression

Evidence Check

Remove retrieved passages, trace which layers used which tokens, audit memory writes, separate observation from summary, perturb weak labels, test cross-modal disagreement, and check whether confidence drops when real support is removed.

Failure Boundary

The method fails when contribution scores confuse correlation with support, when reliability weights silence rare but true evidence, or when the memory system writes unsupported conclusions into the place reserved for observations.

Connection

ThinKV asks what context must be kept. VideoKR and Visual Attribution Streaming ask which video evidence caused an answer. Common Corpus and Hubble preserve dataset source records. Information Flow UQ, DLMR, RFCMH, WETR, and AGREE connect those ideas into one rule: memory helps only when it remains evidence with receipts.

EXEMPLAR 81

DR Tulu

Teaching A Research Agent While The Checklist Improves

Problem

When a person begins a hard research task, the first checklist is usually incomplete. After reading sources, they learn that a good answer must mention a caveat, compare a missing baseline, or reject an early assumption. A research agent has the same issue: if training uses only the first checklist, it can learn to satisfy an outdated measure of quality.

State Space

The state is the user question, search path, discovered sources, temporary hypotheses, checklist criteria, agent actions, answer draft, checker feedback, and changes to the reward over time.

Object

The object is two linked learning processes. One process improves what the agent does: where it searches, which sources it trusts, when it revises a draft, and when it stops. The other process improves the checklist used to judge that work. The dangerous part is that the checklist is also data: if it learns from weak drafts, it can start rewarding weak habits. In formal language, the agent's action rule is a policy, the checklist score is a reward, and learning behavior from that score is reinforcement learning.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is the real research obligation: answer the question using the best evidence found so far. The checklist can change, but it should move toward that obligation rather than toward whatever the current agent happens to produce easily.

Allowed Movement

What may change is the checklist, the required evidence, the subclaims, and the agent's search strategy. Those changes are allowed only when grounded in sources or verified failures, not in style preferences or self-confirming drafts.

Why It Works

It works when checklist changes are treated like claims that need evidence. The agent searches; new sources reveal that the old checklist missed a necessary caveat or comparison; the checklist is revised; then the next training step gives a sharper signal. The method fails in spirit if the checklist changes merely because the agent found an easy style that scores well. Then the agent and judge can agree with each other while both move away from the outside facts. Name used later: co-evolving rubric and policy means the checklist and action rule change together, so every checklist movement needs an audit trail.

agent improves when the score changes because new evidence demands it, not because the agent learned to flatter the score

Evidence Check

Compare fixed-checklist and evolving-checklist training, inspect each checklist revision, verify that new criteria came from external evidence, test on held-out research questions, and measure whether answers improve on facts or caveats not visible at the first checklist step.

Failure Boundary

The approach fails when checklist updates reward length, source count, confidence, or self-confirming claims instead of evidence quality. It also fails when the agent learns to write material that bends future checklists in its favor.

Connection

RLVepsR studies what happens when checker feedback is noisy. Omni-Reward and JudgeBench treat reward models as imperfect measuring channels. ATLAS changes evaluation questions as uncertainty changes. DR Tulu is the research-agent version: the measuring rule may move, but the reason it moved must be visible.

EXEMPLAR 82

SceneSmith, MomaGraph, EgoTactile, And TimeRewarder

Giving A Robot The Facts That Make An Action Possible

Problem

A photo can show a cup on a table, but it may not show whether the cup is slippery, whether the handle faces the robot, whether there is room to move, or whether the last attempt actually worked. A robot fails when its data describes the scene in words or pixels but omits the facts that decide whether an action can happen.

State Space

The state is the room or scene, object identities, where objects are, how they relate, what actions each object allows, hand or body pose, touch and pressure, human demonstration video, evidence of success, and the rule for how the world changes after an action.

Object

The object is a usable action state: a working description that contains the facts needed to choose and verify a physical move. A robot does not only need the word "cup." It needs where the cup is, whether it can be grasped, what blocks the path, what touching it feels like, and what counts as success after the action. SceneSmith creates indoor scenes for practice. MomaGraph stores objects and what they can do to each other as a graph. EgoTactile adds contact facts that sight misses. TimeRewarder turns human videos into evidence about whether an action actually succeeded.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is action-relevant reality. The representation has to keep the variables that decide whether the robot can grasp, push, open, place, or avoid something, and whether the result matched the goal.

Allowed Movement

What may change is how the state is built. The pipeline can generate scenes, simplify them into object-relation graphs, add touch sensors, or infer success from video. But it must keep the physical agreement between state, action, contact, and outcome.

Why It Works

It works because the learning signal is moved closer to the physical event that matters. Pixels can show that a hand is near a handle, but they may not show whether the fingers made contact, whether the handle turned, or whether the door opened far enough. Scene generation helps only if the scenes obey the physical rules the robot will later face. Graphs help only if their links match real ways objects constrain or enable each other. Touch helps because contact is often the missing fact between a planned action and a real outcome. Video-derived reward helps when it marks success over time instead of rewarding a single nice-looking frame. Name used later: partially observed control problem means acting while important state facts are hidden.

usable robot data = scene facts + object relations + touch/contact + success evidence for state -> action -> next state

Evidence Check

Test stale graphs, slipping contact, impossible generated scenes, wrong success labels from video, transfer from simulation to real robots, cases where language planning succeeds but execution fails, and whether changing affordance or contact facts changes the policy's action.

Failure Boundary

The approach fails when generated scenes break physics, graphs omit a relation that matters for the task, touch signals are delayed or miscalibrated, or video reward learns visual style instead of real success.

Connection

SAW-Bench and RoboMME measure embodied memory and reasoning. HDFlow, PACT, JoSE, and RodriNet constrain physical moves. BehaviorVLA and DreamDojo connect practice to deployment. SceneSmith, MomaGraph, EgoTactile, and TimeRewarder state the prerequisite: the policy needs a view of the world where action validity is visible.

EXEMPLAR 83

FlashVID, InfoTok, VideoFlexTok, OmniFit, And LST

Keeping The Few Moments That Can Change The Answer

Problem

Imagine skimming a long security video to answer one question: did anyone pick up the bag? Most frames show nothing changing, but one half-second matters. If the summary deletes that moment, the summary is cheap but useless. Video, speech, and mixed media give models the same problem: there is too much input to keep everything, but the small part that matters may be brief.

State Space

The state is the raw video frames, sounds or speech, text, repeated information inside each medium, the token budget, the latency budget, the kept summary, the later question, and the error caused by throwing information away.

Object

The object is a task-preserving summary under a budget. Think of making notes from a long meeting when you do not yet know which question your boss will ask. You can drop repeated small talk, but you must keep any moment that could change an answer: who agreed, who objected, when a decision changed, or which slide contradicted the speaker. FlashVID merges repeated visual evidence. InfoTok and VideoFlexTok spend video tokens on moments that carry more answer-changing information. OmniFit decides how to divide limited tokens across media and model layers. LST turns long speech into compact internal chunks while still allowing new audio to arrive. Name used later: an approximate sufficient statistic is a smaller record that keeps almost all task-needed evidence.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is answer-relevant evidence. If a sound, gesture, frame, or cross-media clue would change the answer, the compressed state must keep it or keep a reliable sign of it.

Allowed Movement

What may change is the storage form. The system may merge repeated frames, drop easy parts, pool nearby tokens, patch speech into chunks, or move budget between media. Each move should be judged by the uncertainty it creates, not by token savings alone.

Why It Works

It works when the summary makes the discarded material unable to change the answer. That is the core test. If the kept record leads to the same answer as the full stream, the discarded frames or words were extra for that task. If a removed half-second would change the answer, the method did not become efficient; it threw away evidence. The hard part is that importance depends on the later question. A quiet frame can be useless for "what color is the car?" and essential for "did the driver look down before turning?" Streaming adds another burden: the model must keep unresolved clues that may only become meaningful after later speech, sound, or video arrives.

a compact stream is valid when answering from it is nearly the same as answering from the full stream under the token budget

Evidence Check

Try many token budgets, rare events, late questions, before-after order questions, conflicts between sound and image, latency-sensitive speech, and removals of tokens that the method claimed were low value.

Failure Boundary

The method fails when it cannot know what future question will be asked, when a short decisive moment looks locally ordinary, or when it assumes two media say the same thing even though only one of them contains the hidden fact.

Connection

ThinKV, STAR-KV, and EntroKV keep the text context needed later. Generative Filtering keeps a hidden belief state. DLMR keeps source labels in memory. FlashVID, InfoTok, VideoFlexTok, OmniFit, and LST extend the same principle to many media: efficiency is correct only when the kept summary can still answer the task.

EXEMPLAR 84

NextStep-1, EditVerse, LatentLM, And UALM

Editing Media While Keeping It Usable For The Next Step

Problem

Suppose you ask an editor to change a video so the car is red, then later ask them to make the same car turn left and add matching engine sound. The first edit must leave behind a usable object for the second edit. A generator that treats text, image, video, and audio as one flat string can lose the details that make later edits precise.

State Space

The state is the instruction, a shared editing language, the medium's own hidden state for image, audio, or video, the current artifact, the part to edit, the parts that must not change, the decoder that turns hidden state into media, and the context for the next instruction.

Object

The object is a shared workflow rule for many media. A useful editor needs two layers. One layer says what the user wants: keep the same car, make it red, preserve the street, add matching sound. The other layer stores the artifact in the form that can actually be edited: image layout, audio timing, video motion, or a compact media state. NextStep-1 treats creation and editing as predicting the next usable step. EditVerse puts text, image, and video actions into one editing sequence. LatentLM emits compressed media states learned by a compressor-and-reconstructor called an autoencoder. VAE latent vectors are hidden compressed number lists for that media state. UALM links audio understanding, audio generation, text reasoning, and mixed-media reasoning through one interface.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is controllable artifact identity: the same object, layout, timing, sound structure, edit target, and enough internal state to keep editing later.

Allowed Movement

What may change is the requested part of the artifact and the format used to represent it. The model can translate between media, produce native image or audio states, or edit an existing artifact. It must separate the coordinates that should change from the facts that should survive.

Why It Works

It works when control and validity are kept separate but connected. The instruction layer carries what should change and what must stay fixed. The media layer carries what makes the artifact real enough to render or play: image positions, audio timing, waveform shape, video identity, and motion. If the model stores only the final pixels or sound, later edits have to guess what the object was. If it stores only an abstract instruction, the output may lose detail.
